    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.

I used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.

So… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.

Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.

Fishing, going through old vinyl records—there’s this one crate I keep meaning to organize, and I used to love doing that on a Sunday.\n\nAnd tailgating, too, or checking on my home-brew… not that I’m some expert or anything, my cousin acts like he is, he’ll talk your ear off about hops—but lately all of it just feels like another chore. Like I should care, but I don’t really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, I think. My jeans have been looser, and most days I kind of have to remind myself to eat. Supper will be sitting there and it just looks like… too much trouble, if that makes sense.\n\nI used to be the guy who’d snack while watching TV, or make a proper plate, but lately I’ll look at it and end up pushing it around. So yeah, I guess I’ve dropped some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just kind of sitting there longer than I mean to, but I couldn’t tell you if I’m moving slower or if I’m just not paying attention.\n\nRestless… maybe I get up and wander to the fridge or the window, but then I forget what I was doing. My dad used to do that when he was looking for his keys—he had this little dish by the door and still never used it. So, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months, I’d say. Since that crash, really. I kept thinking it would lift after a week or two, like when the weather changes and everybody gets in a mood, but it just sort of stayed.\n\nThree months sounds about right, though. I’m bad with dates—my wife used to keep the calendar on the fridge, little magnets and all that—but yeah, this low spell’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess ashamed is the word. Not guilty about, like, one specific thing exactly, more like after that big burst of energy I had wore off, it felt like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, and I don’t know, it just felt empty. Like I’d let people down somehow, even if I couldn’t point to a single thing. My mother used to say coffee fixes most mornings, but it doesn’t really do much when you’re just sitting there feeling like that. So… yeah, ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.\n\nSo… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.

I’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. Funny, I used to buy this cheap diner coffee because it reminded me of this place near where I worked years ago—had the worst pancakes but everybody went there anyway. But lately it’s not like that. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.

So I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.

My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Not really completely, no. I might have an hour here or there where I’m distracted enough to seem normal, but after that… it’s still there.

After that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, just feeling empty. I kept thinking it would lift after a week or two, like when the weather changes and everybody gets in a mood, but it just sort of stayed.\n\nThree months sounds about right, though. I’m bad with dates—my wife used to keep the calendar on the fridge, little magnets and all that—but yeah, this low spell’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess ashamed is the word. Not guilty about, like, one specific thing exactly, more like after that big burst of energy I had wore off, it felt like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, and I don’t know, it just felt empty. Like I’d let people down somehow, even if I couldn’t point to a single thing. My mother used to say coffee fixes most mornings, but it doesn’t really do much when you’re just sitting there feeling like that. So… yeah, ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.\n\nSo… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.\n\nI’ll think, “Maybe I should put a record on,” and then I’m just standing there looking at the shelf like it’s laundry I forgot to fold. Funny, because I used to know exactly which album I wanted depending on the weather. But lately, no… I’ve kind of quit caring about all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're at a low point most days, or are there still some days that are better than others?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. Funny, I used to buy this cheap diner coffee because it reminded me of this place near where I worked years ago—had the worst pancakes but everybody went there anyway. But lately it’s not like that. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.\n\nSo I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not really completely, no. Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.

After that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.

So… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.

Fishing, going through old vinyl records—there’s this one crate I keep meaning to organize, and I used to love doing that on a Sunday.\n\nAnd tailgating, too, or checking on my home-brew… not that I’m some expert or anything, my cousin acts like he is, he’ll talk your ear off about hops—but lately all of it just feels like another chore. Like I should care, but I don’t really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, I think. My jeans have been looser, and most days I kind of have to remind myself to eat. Supper will be sitting there and it just looks like… too much trouble, if that makes sense.\n\nI used to be the guy who’d snack while watching TV, or make a proper plate, but lately I’ll look at it and end up pushing it around. So yeah, I guess I’ve dropped some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just kind of sitting there longer than I mean to, but I couldn’t tell you if I’m moving slower or if I’m just not paying attention.\n\nRestless… maybe I get up and wander to the fridge or the window, but then I forget what I was doing. My dad used to do that when he was looking for his keys—he had this little dish by the door and still never used it. So, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months, I’d say. Since that crash, really. I kept thinking it would lift after a week or two, like when the weather changes and everybody gets in a mood, but it just sort of stayed.\n\nThree months sounds about right, though. I’m bad with dates—my wife used to keep the calendar on the fridge, little magnets and all that—but yeah, this low spell’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess ashamed is the word. Not guilty about, like, one specific thing exactly, more like after that big burst of energy I had wore off, it felt like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, and I don’t know, it just felt empty. Like I’d let people down somehow, even if I couldn’t point to a single thing. My mother used to say coffee fixes most mornings, but it doesn’t really do much when you’re just sitting there feeling like that. So… yeah, ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.\n\nSo… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.\n\nI’ll think, “Maybe I should put a record on,” and then I’m just standing there looking at the shelf like it’s laundry I forgot to fold. Funny, because I used to know exactly which album I wanted depending on the weather. But lately, no… I’ve kind of quit caring about all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're at a low point most days, or are there still some days that are better than others?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. Funny, I used to buy this cheap diner coffee because it reminded me of this place near where I worked years ago—had the worst pancakes but everybody went there anyway. But lately it’s not like that. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.\n\nSo I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not really completely, no. I might have an hour here or there where I’m distracted enough to seem normal, but after that… it’s still there.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is a funny thing because I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed about. My dad used to say coffee fixes most things—terrible advice, really—but I keep making it anyway.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a day where I felt fully like myself. Just maybe less bad for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or struggled with similar feelings before in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.\n\nSo… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I think so. Not exactly like this, but I’ve had more than one stretch where I got really low.

This time, after that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the table with coffee in front of me, just staring at it like it was supposed to tell me something. I wouldn’t even drink it half the time. Just felt empty… ashamed, too.

Coffee used to be kind of a little routine for me, you know—same mug, same seat—but lately it just sits there getting cold. So, yes, I’ve had lows before, but this one’s the one that’s been sitting heavy on me.

Just maybe less bad for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or struggled with similar feelings before in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.\n\nSo… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not exactly like this, but I’ve had more than one stretch where I got really low.\n\nThis time, after that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the table with coffee in front of me, just staring at it like it was supposed to tell me something. I wouldn’t even drink it half the time. Just felt empty… ashamed, too.\n\nCoffee used to be kind of a little routine for me, you know—same mug, same seat—but lately it just sits there getting cold. So, yes, I’ve had lows before, but this one’s the one that’s been sitting heavy on me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been diagnosed with depression or received treatment for it before this episode?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I ever sat down with somebody and had them say, “This is depression,” not that I remember anyway.

Treatment-wise, I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about being worn down or not sleeping right, but that was usually in between work schedules and bills and all that. Retail hours will mess with anybody, you know—one week mornings, next week closing, and then you’re eating dinner at 10:30 like that’s normal.

So… no clear diagnosis before this, I guess. Not one I can point to.

Once I crashed into the low spell, the voice stopped and I was left embarrassed by what I had believed.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on bills, missed callbacks and appointments, and could not keep a steady job search going even though I needed work. My usual routines at home got sloppy enough that I knew I could not just handle it by willpower.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: That high-energy, out-of-control stretch lasted about three weeks after I was fired.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The low spell has been hanging on for about three months since that crash.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or struggled with similar feelings before in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.\n\nSo… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’d sit at the table with coffee in front of me, just staring at it like it was supposed to tell me something. I wouldn’t even drink it half the time. Just felt empty… ashamed, too.\n\nCoffee used to be kind of a little routine for me, you know—same mug, same seat—but lately it just sits there getting cold. So, yes, I’ve had lows before, but this one’s the one that’s been sitting heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression or received treatment for it before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I ever sat down with somebody and had them say, “This is depression,” not that I remember anyway.\n\nTreatment-wise, I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about being worn down or not sleeping right, but that was usually in between work schedules and bills and all that. Yeah… there was that stretch after I got fired where I felt a lot better than this. “Better” is maybe not the right word, because it got kind of out of hand, but compared to sitting around feeling low, yeah, I had a lot of energy.

Like I’ll be getting ready for work or lying there before sleep, and the thought pops up that maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made any plan to hurt myself or anything. It kind of came after that burst of energy wore off, like somebody pulled the plug on me. So yeah, down. Most of the time lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have little things that would kind of settle me down, you know? Fishing, going through old vinyl records—there’s this one crate I keep meaning to organize, and I used to love doing that on a Sunday.\n\nAnd tailgating, too, or checking on my home-brew… not that I’m some expert or anything, my cousin acts like he is, he’ll talk your ear off about hops—but lately all of it just feels like another chore. Like I should care, but I don’t really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, I think. My jeans have been looser, and most days I kind of have to remind myself to eat. Supper will be sitting there and it just looks like… too much trouble, if that makes sense.\n\nI used to be the guy who’d snack while watching TV, or make a proper plate, but lately I’ll look at it and end up pushing it around. So yeah, I guess I’ve dropped some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just kind of sitting there longer than I mean to, but I couldn’t tell you if I’m moving slower or if I’m just not paying attention.\n\nRestless… maybe I get up and wander to the fridge or the window, but then I forget what I was doing. My dad used to do that when he was looking for his keys—he had this little dish by the door and still never used it. So, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months, I’d say. Since that crash, really. I kept thinking it would lift after a week or two, like when the weather changes and everybody gets in a mood, but it just sort of stayed.\n\nThree months sounds about right, though. I’m bad with dates—my wife used to keep the calendar on the fridge, little magnets and all that—but yeah, this low spell’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess ashamed is the word. Not guilty about, like, one specific thing exactly, more like after that big burst of energy I had wore off, it felt like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, and I don’t know, it just felt empty. Like I’d let people down somehow, even if I couldn’t point to a single thing. My mother used to say coffee fixes most mornings, but it doesn’t really do much when you’re just sitting there feeling like that. So… yeah, ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.\n\nSo… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.\n\nI’ll think, “Maybe I should put a record on,” and then I’m just standing there looking at the shelf like it’s laundry I forgot to fold. Funny, because I used to know exactly which album I wanted depending on the weather. But lately, no… I’ve kind of quit caring about all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're at a low point most days, or are there still some days that are better than others?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.\n\nSo I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not really completely, no. I might have an hour here or there where I’m distracted enough to seem normal, but after that… it’s still there.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is a funny thing because I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed about. It’s more like… the thought keeps catching me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, I think. It kind of came after that burst of energy wore off, like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d just sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it’s funny, I used to make coffee for everyone at the store because I knew exactly who wanted sugar and who didn’t—anyway… I’d just sit there feeling empty, and ashamed, I guess. So yeah, down. Most of the time lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have little things that would kind of settle me down, you know? Fishing, going through old vinyl records—there’s this one crate I keep meaning to organize, and I used to love doing that on a Sunday.\n\nAnd tailgating, too, or checking on my home-brew… not that I’m some expert or anything, my cousin acts like he is, he’ll talk your ear off about hops—but lately all of it just feels like another chore. Like I should care, but I don’t really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, I think. My jeans have been looser, and most days I kind of have to remind myself to eat. Supper will be sitting there and it just looks like… too much trouble, if that makes sense.\n\nI used to be the guy who’d snack while watching TV, or make a proper plate, but lately I’ll look at it and end up pushing it around. So yeah, I guess I’ve dropped some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just kind of sitting there longer than I mean to, but I couldn’t tell you if I’m moving slower or if I’m just not paying attention.\n\nRestless… maybe I get up and wander to the fridge or the window, but then I forget what I was doing. My dad used to do that when he was looking for his keys—he had this little dish by the door and still never used it. So, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months, I’d say. Since that crash, really. I kept thinking it would lift after a week or two, like when the weather changes and everybody gets in a mood, but it just sort of stayed.\n\nThree months sounds about right, though. I’m bad with dates—my wife used to keep the calendar on the fridge, little magnets and all that—but yeah, this low spell’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess ashamed is the word. Not guilty about, like, one specific thing exactly, more like after that big burst of energy I had wore off, it felt like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, and I don’t know, it just felt empty. Like I’d let people down somehow, even if I couldn’t point to a single thing. My mother used to say coffee fixes most mornings, but it doesn’t really do much when you’re just sitting there feeling like that. So… yeah, ashamed, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, the stuff I used to kind of count on… fishing, looking through my old vinyl records, tailgating, even checking on the home-brew in the garage… it all just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to be able to lose a whole afternoon flipping through records, you know, reading the backs like I was discovering something. My uncle had this old turntable that weighed about as much as a lawnmower—anyway. Now I look at the crates and just think, “I should clean those up,” and then I don’t.\n\nSo… no, I’m not really looking forward to much. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.\n\nI’ll think, “Maybe I should put a record on,” and then I’m just standing there looking at the shelf like it’s laundry I forgot to fold. Funny, because I used to know exactly which album I wanted depending on the weather. But lately, no… I’ve kind of quit caring about all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're at a low point most days, or are there still some days that are better than others?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. Funny, I used to buy this cheap diner coffee because it reminded me of this place near where I worked years ago—had the worst pancakes but everybody went there anyway. But lately it’s not like that. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.\n\nSo I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not really completely, no. I might have an hour here or there where I’m distracted enough to seem normal, but after that… it’s still there.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is a funny thing because I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed about. My dad used to say coffee fixes most things—terrible advice, really—but I keep making it anyway.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a day where I felt fully like myself. Just maybe less bad for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or struggled with similar feelings before in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.\n\nSo… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not exactly like this, but I’ve had more than one stretch where I got really low.\n\nThis time, after that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the table with coffee in front of me, just staring at it like it was supposed to tell me something. I wouldn’t even drink it half the time. Just felt empty… ashamed, too.\n\nCoffee used to be kind of a little routine for me, you know—same mug, same seat—but lately it just sits there getting cold. So, yes, I’ve had lows before, but this one’s the one that’s been sitting heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression or received treatment for it before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I ever sat down with somebody and had them say, “This is depression,” not that I remember anyway.\n\nTreatment-wise, I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about being worn down or not sleeping right, but that was usually in between work schedules and bills and all that. Retail hours will mess with anybody, you know—one week mornings, next week closing, and then you’re eating dinner at 10:30 like that’s normal.\n\nSo… no clear diagnosis before this, I guess. Not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, and if so, how long did those periods last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was that stretch after I got fired where I felt a lot better than this. “Better” is maybe not the right word, because it got kind of out of hand, but compared to sitting around feeling low, yeah, I had a lot of energy.\n\nIt lasted… about three weeks, I think. Funny thing is, I remember it by the grocery store schedule more than the calendar, because I kept thinking I’d use all that time to get organized, clean out the garage, maybe fix the shelf in the hallway that’s been leaning forever. But yeah—about three weeks after I lost the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I’ve had times before where I felt low, but this one stands out. After that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee—same mug I always use, though I don’t know why that matters—and I’d just stare at it. Not even drink it sometimes. Just this empty feeling, and… shame, I guess. Like I’d done something wrong, even sitting there.\n\nSo, more than once, probably, but this one felt heavier.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even the things that used to settle me down feel like chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Maybe little moments here and there, but honestly… not much. The usual things that settle me down just don’t do it anymore. Fishing, going through my vinyl records, tailgating, checking on the home-brew in the garage… they all feel like chores now.\n\nI’ll think, “Maybe I should put a record on,” and then I’m just standing there looking at the shelf like it’s laundry I forgot to fold. Funny, because I used to know exactly which album I wanted depending on the weather. But lately, no… I’ve kind of quit caring about all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're at a low point most days, or are there still some days that are better than others?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah… it feels like a low point. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even really drinking it, just… sitting there. Funny, I used to buy this cheap diner coffee because it reminded me of this place near where I worked years ago—had the worst pancakes but everybody went there anyway. But lately it’s not like that. It’s just me sitting there feeling empty, and ashamed for reasons I can’t quite pin down.\n\nSo I guess there might be a day that’s a little less bad, but mostly it’s been low.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not really completely, no. I might have an hour here or there where I’m distracted enough to seem normal, but after that… it’s still there.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is a funny thing because I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed about. My dad used to say coffee fixes most things—terrible advice, really—but I keep making it anyway.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a day where I felt fully like myself. Just maybe less bad for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or struggled with similar feelings before in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve had bad patches before, sure. Everybody does, I guess. But this one feels… I don’t know, flatter.\n\nAfter that big burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d end up sitting at the table just staring into my coffee, not even drinking it, feeling empty and kind of ashamed. And the coffee thing is funny because I used to be real particular about it—had this mug from a gas station in Ohio, cracked handle and all—but now I just sit there with it going cold.\n\nSo… similar feelings maybe, but this low feels different. Harder to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not exactly like this, but I’ve had more than one stretch where I got really low.\n\nThis time, after that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the table with coffee in front of me, just staring at it like it was supposed to tell me something. I wouldn’t even drink it half the time. Just felt empty… ashamed, too.\n\nCoffee used to be kind of a little routine for me, you know—same mug, same seat—but lately it just sits there getting cold. So, yes, I’ve had lows before, but this one’s the one that’s been sitting heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression or received treatment for it before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I ever sat down with somebody and had them say, “This is depression,” not that I remember anyway.\n\nTreatment-wise, I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about being worn down or not sleeping right, but that was usually in between work schedules and bills and all that. Retail hours will mess with anybody, you know—one week mornings, next week closing, and then you’re eating dinner at 10:30 like that’s normal.\n\nSo… no clear diagnosis before this, I guess. Not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now, and if so, how long did those periods last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was that stretch after I got fired where I felt a lot better than this. “Better” is maybe not the right word, because it got kind of out of hand, but compared to sitting around feeling low, yeah, I had a lot of energy.\n\nIt lasted… about three weeks, I think. Funny thing is, I remember it by the grocery store schedule more than the calendar, because I kept thinking I’d use all that time to get organized, clean out the garage, maybe fix the shelf in the hallway that’s been leaning forever. But yeah—about three weeks after I lost the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I’ve had times before where I felt low, but this one stands out. After that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody just pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee—same mug I always use, though I don’t know why that matters—and I’d just stare at it. Not even drink it sometimes. Just this empty feeling, and… shame, I guess. Like I’d done something wrong, even sitting there.\n\nSo, more than once, probably, but this one felt heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more episodes of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah… though I’m bad at counting them like they’re separate boxes on a form.\n\nThis last one, after that big burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d just sit at the table staring at my coffee, not even really drinking it, feeling empty and ashamed. I’ve had low times before too, but that’s the one I keep coming back to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
